Yes, I have. It’s been that way for a long time now, honestly. Fishing used to be something I’d look forward to—getting the gear ready, checking the weather, all that. It’s more this gray, flat feeling that’s just been hanging around most days for a long time.\n\nLike, I used to really look forward to fishing, or working on the tomatoes out back—my neighbor swears by this one fertilizer, but I don’t know, it smells awful—and even church concerts, I’d enjoy those. Lately those things just feel more like… well, like items on a checklist. I do them, or I think about doing them, but the enjoyment isn’t really there the way it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, like fishing, gardening, or attending church concerts?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. It’s been that way for a long time now, honestly. Fishing used to be something I’d look forward to—getting the gear ready, checking the weather, all that. And the tomatoes out back, I used to fuss over them more than I needed to. Now it’s more like… I know those are things I’m supposed to enjoy, so I do them or think about doing them, but it just feels flat.\n\nSame with a church concert. I’ll go, and I can tell it’s nice, but it doesn’t really land the way it used to. It’s like I’m checking it off a list instead of actually enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the low mood and loss of interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess if I had to put a time on it… it’s been about two years and four months. It really seemed to start when I got pulled back into full operational leadership at work.\n\nI mean, I’d been doing more planning and oversight before that, and then suddenly it was all the day-to-day fires again—servers, staffing, budgets, people needing answers right now. Funny thing is, I used to be pretty good at that kind of pressure. But around then is when this low, flat feeling started settling in, and it’s just kind of stayed there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant decrease in your energy levels or an increase in fatigue during the day, even if you’ve slept well?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually a big part of why I came in. I’m tired in a way that doesn’t quite add up.\n\nI mean, I can sleep, and I’m still getting through work, answering emails, keeping things moving—though don’t get me started on how many “urgent” tickets aren’t really urgent—but even before the day really gets started, I feel like I’m already on a low battery. Not completely wiped out, exactly, but like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over the past couple of years—like eating more or less than usual or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. My appetite’s been off more days than not. I’ll get busy at work and just work straight through lunch without really noticing, which I used to never do—well, unless there was some big system outage, and there’s always somebody who thinks their printer not working is an outage, you know.\n\nBut then by dinner, even if my wife makes something I normally like, I’ll just kind of pick at it. She’s actually the one who pointed out my pants are fitting looser. I’m not trying to lose weight or anything, it just sort of happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or like you’re not measuring up at work or home more often than not?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, work always gives you plenty of opportunities to feel like you’re not doing enough—there’s always some system that needs patching or some meeting that should’ve been an email.\n\nBut guilt… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed myself sitting around thinking I’m a failure or anything like that. Maybe I get frustrated with myself sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling more restless or unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: Hmm. I don’t think so? Not in any obvious way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I’m probably not exactly zipping around like I did when I was younger, but that could just be age, or sitting at a desk too long. And speaking slowly… my wife would probably say I still talk plenty when I get going, especially if it’s about work nonsense or why software updates always break the one thing nobody tested.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed feeling unable to sit still or moving noticeably slower. I haven’t really noticed myself sitting around thinking I’m a failure or anything like that. Not something that’s jumped out at me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that others have commented on you seeming slowed down or, on the contrary, unusually restless or agitated lately?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think anyone’s said that to me. My wife might say I look tired sometimes, but slowed down or agitated—no, not really.\n\nAt work, people comment on all kinds of things, usually when something’s broken five minutes before a meeting, but I can’t think of anyone saying I’m moving slow or acting restless. My wife might say I look tired sometimes, but slowed down or agitated—no, not really.\n\nAt work, people comment on all kinds of things, usually when something’s broken five minutes before a meeting, but I can’t think of anyone saying I’m moving slow or acting restless. So… no, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like life isn’t worth living or had thoughts about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, I don’t think so. Yeah, sleep’s been… not great, I guess. I usually fall asleep okay, so it’s not like I’m lying there for hours at the start.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around crying or anything like that, but there’s been this sort of gray, flat feeling most days for a long time now.

It’s like even things I used to look forward to don’t really land the same. Fishing, for example—I used to get the gear ready the night before, check the weather, all that. Now it’s more like, “Well, I said I was going, so I guess I’ll go.” Same with tending the tomatoes or going to a church concert. Those should be nice things, and they are, technically, but they feel more like items on a checklist than something I’m really enjoying.

So… most days, yes. It’s just been kind of muted.

Sometimes lower than half, honestly. It doesn’t quite make sense to me, because I’m not completely falling apart or anything. I’m functioning. I just feel like I’m pushing through from the minute the day gets going.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days, even if it's been mild? It could be a sense of sadness, emptiness, or just not feeling like yourself.\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m crying all the time or anything dramatic like that. It’s more this gray, flat feeling that’s just been hanging around most days for a long time.\n\nLike, I used to really look forward to fishing, or working on the tomatoes out back—my neighbor swears by this one fertilizer, but I don’t know, it smells awful—and even church concerts, I’d enjoy those. Lately those things just feel more like… well, like items on a checklist. I do them, or I think about doing them, but the enjoyment isn’t really there the way it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, like fishing, gardening, or attending church concerts?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. It’s been that way for a long time now, honestly. Fishing used to be something I’d look forward to—getting the gear ready, checking the weather, all that. And the tomatoes out back, I used to fuss over them more than I needed to. Now it’s more like… I know those are things I’m supposed to enjoy, so I do them or think about doing them, but it just feels flat.\n\nSame with a church concert. I’ll go, and I can tell it’s nice, but it doesn’t really land the way it used to. It’s like I’m checking it off a list instead of actually enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the low mood and loss of interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess if I had to put a time on it… it’s been about two years and four months. It really seemed to start when I got pulled back into full operational leadership at work.\n\nI mean, I’d been doing more planning and oversight before that, and then suddenly it was all the day-to-day fires again—servers, staffing, budgets, people needing answers right now. Funny thing is, I used to be pretty good at that kind of pressure. But around then is when this low, flat feeling started settling in, and it’s just kind of stayed there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant decrease in your energy levels or an increase in fatigue during the day, even if you’ve slept well?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually a big part of why I came in. I’m tired in a way that doesn’t quite add up.\n\nI mean, I can sleep, and I’m still getting through work, answering emails, keeping things moving—though don’t get me started on how many “urgent” tickets aren’t really urgent—but even before the day really gets started, I feel like I’m already on a low battery. Not completely wiped out, exactly, but like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over the past couple of years—like eating more or less than usual or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. My appetite’s been off more days than not. She’s actually the one who pointed out my pants are fitting looser. I’m not trying to lose weight or anything, it just sort of happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or like you’re not measuring up at work or home more often than not?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way, exactly. Yeah… I guess I have, though I didn’t really think of it as a “weight loss” thing at first.

My appetite’s been kind of off more days than not. I'm Dr. [Name]. Can you tell me what's been troubling you lately that brought you here today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly I came in because I’m just… tired. Not like I stayed up too late tired, exactly. I can sleep, and I’m still getting through work—computer systems stuff, there’s always some server or budget thing—but it’s like I start the day with the battery already half-drained. Sometimes lower than half, honestly. It doesn’t quite make sense to me, because I’m not completely falling apart or anything. I’m functioning. I just feel like I’m pushing through from the minute the day gets going.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days, even if it's been mild? It could be a sense of sadness, emptiness, or just not feeling like yourself.\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m crying all the time or anything dramatic like that. It’s more this gray, flat feeling that’s just been hanging around most days for a long time.\n\nLike, I used to really look forward to fishing, or working on the tomatoes out back—my neighbor swears by this one fertilizer, but I don’t know, it smells awful—and even church concerts, I’d enjoy those. Lately those things just feel more like… well, like items on a checklist. I do them, or I think about doing them, but the enjoyment isn’t really there the way it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, like fishing, gardening, or attending church concerts?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. It’s been that way for a long time now, honestly. Fishing used to be something I’d look forward to—getting the gear ready, checking the weather, all that. And the tomatoes out back, I used to fuss over them more than I needed to. Now it’s more like… I know those are things I’m supposed to enjoy, so I do them or think about doing them, but it just feels flat.\n\nSame with a church concert. I’ll go, and I can tell it’s nice, but it doesn’t really land the way it used to. It’s like I’m checking it off a list instead of actually enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the low mood and loss of interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess if I had to put a time on it… it’s been about two years and four months. It really seemed to start when I got pulled back into full operational leadership at work.\n\nI mean, I’d been doing more planning and oversight before that, and then suddenly it was all the day-to-day fires again—servers, staffing, budgets, people needing answers right now. Funny thing is, I used to be pretty good at that kind of pressure. But around then is when this low, flat feeling started settling in, and it’s just kind of stayed there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a significant decrease in your energy levels or an increase in fatigue during the day, even if you’ve slept well?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s actually a big part of why I came in. I’m tired in a way that doesn’t quite add up.\n\nI mean, I can sleep, and I’m still getting through work, answering emails, keeping things moving—though don’t get me started on how many “urgent” tickets aren’t really urgent—but even before the day really gets started, I feel like I’m already on a low battery. Not completely wiped out, exactly, but like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over the past couple of years—like eating more or less than usual or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. My appetite’s been off more days than not. I’ll get busy at work and just work straight through lunch without really noticing, which I used to never do—well, unless there was some big system outage, and there’s always somebody who thinks their printer not working is an outage, you know.\n\nBut then by dinner, even if my wife makes something I normally like, I’ll just kind of pick at it. She’s actually the one who pointed out my pants are fitting looser. I’m not trying to lose weight or anything, it just sort of happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or like you’re not measuring up at work or home more often than not?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way, exactly. Not something that’s jumped out at me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that others have commented on you seeming slowed down or, on the contrary, unusually restless or agitated lately?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think anyone’s said that to me. I don’t think I’ve been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep—like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or sleeping more than usual even when you don’t feel tired?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been… not great, I guess. It’s already sorting through some server issue, or a compliance deadline, or some ridiculous thing from work where nobody reads the email until it’s too late. And then I’m just lying there, half-awake, waiting for the alarm.\n\nSo technically I slept, but it doesn’t really feel like the night counted, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood and the lack of enjoyment in things have been present most of the day, nearly every day, for the past several months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around crying or anything like that, but there’s been this sort of gray, flat feeling most days for a long time now.\n\nIt’s like even things I used to look forward to don’t really land the same. Fishing, for example—I used to get the gear ready the night before, check the weather, all that. Now it’s more like, “Well, I said I was going, so I guess I’ll go.” Same with tending the tomatoes or going to a church concert. Those should be nice things, and they are, technically, but they feel more like items on a checklist than something I’m really enjoying.\n\nSo… most days, yes. It’s just been kind of muted.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant weight loss or a decrease in your appetite over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, though I didn’t really think of it as a “weight loss” thing at first.\n\nMy appetite’s been kind of off more days than not. I’ll get tied up with something at work and just work straight through lunch without really noticing, which is easy to do when you’ve got three different people asking why something won’t log in and nobody remembers what they changed. Then dinner comes around and I’ll sit there and kind of pick at it.\n\nMy wife noticed before I did, honestly. She said my pants were looking looser, and I’m not trying to lose weight or anything. I’m tired in a way that doesn’t quite add up.\n\nI mean, I can sleep, and I’m still getting through work, answering emails, keeping things moving—though don’t get me started on how many “urgent” tickets aren’t really urgent—but even before the day really gets started, I feel like I’m already on a low battery. Not completely wiped out, exactly, but like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over the past couple of years—like eating more or less than usual or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah, somewhat. My appetite’s been off more days than not. I’ll get busy at work and just work straight through lunch without really noticing, which I used to never do—well, unless there was some big system outage, and there’s always somebody who thinks their printer not working is an outage, you know.\n\nBut then by dinner, even if my wife makes something I normally like, I’ll just kind of pick at it. She’s actually the one who pointed out my pants are fitting looser. I’m not trying to lose weight or anything, it just sort of happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or like you’re not measuring up at work or home more often than not?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, work always gives you plenty of opportunities to feel like you’re not doing enough—there’s always some system that needs patching or some meeting that should’ve been an email.\n\nBut guilt… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed myself sitting around thinking I’m a failure or anything like that. Maybe I get frustrated with myself sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling more restless or unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: Hmm. I don’t think so? Not in any obvious way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I’m probably not exactly zipping around like I did when I was younger, but that could just be age, or sitting at a desk too long. And speaking slowly… my wife would probably say I still talk plenty when I get going, especially if it’s about work nonsense or why software updates always break the one thing nobody tested.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed feeling unable to sit still or moving noticeably slower. Not something that’s jumped out at me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that others have commented on you seeming slowed down or, on the contrary, unusually restless or agitated lately?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think anyone’s said that to me. My wife might say I look tired sometimes, but slowed down or agitated—no, not really.\n\nAt work, people comment on all kinds of things, usually when something’s broken five minutes before a meeting, but I can’t think of anyone saying I’m moving slow or acting restless. So… no, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like life isn’t worth living or had thoughts about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, I don’t think so. That’s not really where my mind goes.\n\nI can get worn down by things, sure—everybody does, especially when work feels like it’s just one long chain of passwords expiring and people needing things yesterday—but thoughts about harming myself, no. And “life isn’t worth living”… I don’t know, that sounds pretty strong. I don’t think I’ve been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep—like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or sleeping more than usual even when you don’t feel tired?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been… not great, I guess. I usually fall asleep okay, so it’s not like I’m lying there for hours at the start.\n\nBut I’ll wake up around four in the morning, and then my brain just clicks on. It’s already sorting through some server issue, or a compliance deadline, or some ridiculous thing from work where nobody reads the email until it’s too late. And then I’m just lying there, half-awake, waiting for the alarm.\n\nSo technically I slept, but it doesn’t really feel like the night counted, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood and the lack of enjoyment in things have been present most of the day, nearly every day, for the past several months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around crying or anything like that, but there’s been this sort of gray, flat feeling most days for a long time now.\n\nIt’s like even things I used to look forward to don’t really land the same. Fishing, for example—I used to get the gear ready the night before, check the weather, all that. Now it’s more like, “Well, I said I was going, so I guess I’ll go.” Same with tending the tomatoes or going to a church concert. Those should be nice things, and they are, technically, but they feel more like items on a checklist than something I’m really enjoying.\n\nSo… most days, yes. It’s just been kind of muted.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant weight loss or a decrease in your appetite over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, though I didn’t really think of it as a “weight loss” thing at first.\n\nMy appetite’s been kind of off more days than not. I’ll get tied up with something at work and just work straight through lunch without really noticing, which is easy to do when you’ve got three different people asking why something won’t log in and nobody remembers what they changed. Then dinner comes around and I’ll sit there and kind of pick at it.\n\nMy wife noticed before I did, honestly. She said my pants were looking looser, and I’m not trying to lose weight or anything. So… yeah, probably some decrease in appetite and maybe some weight coming off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble focusing, making decisions, or thinking clearly during the day, more often than not?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it trouble thinking clearly, exactly. I mean, I can still do the work, and with computers there’s always some day where you’re staring at a screen thinking, “Why on earth would that setting be there?” Whoever designs some of these systems must not actually use them, you know?\n\nBut… decisions, focusing… I don’t know. Some days I do feel like I’m slower to get started, but I also wonder if that’s just normal at my age or just being tired of the same problems. I haven’t really noticed anything dramatic, I guess. Nothing where I’d say I can’t concentrate at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Completely lost interest… I don’t know if I’d say completely. I still do the things sometimes. I’ll still go fishing, or go out and fuss with the tomatoes, or sit through a church concert if that’s on the calendar.

Yeah… there have been times like that, I suppose. Not like I’m sitting there making some big declaration that fishing is pointless or anything, but it’s more… I look at the rods in the garage, or I think about going out to the tomatoes, and it just feels like another thing I’m supposed to do.

It’s been this gray, flat sort of feeling for a long time now, most days. Even stuff I used to look forward to — fishing, messing with the garden, going to a church concert — it can feel more like checking something off a list than actually enjoying it.

And then I’ll think, well, if I’m just going to stand there and feel the same anyway, why bother? But then sometimes I still go, because it’s on the calendar or someone expects me to.

I’m not trying to lose weight or anything, it just sort of happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty or like you’re not measuring up at work or home more often than not?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it that way, exactly. I mean, work always gives you plenty of opportunities to feel like you’re not doing enough—there’s always some system that needs patching or some meeting that should’ve been an email.\n\nBut guilt… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed myself sitting around thinking I’m a failure or anything like that. Maybe I get frustrated with myself sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling more restless or unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: Hmm. I don’t think so? Not in any obvious way, anyway.\n\nI mean, I’m probably not exactly zipping around like I did when I was younger, but that could just be age, or sitting at a desk too long. And speaking slowly… my wife would probably say I still talk plenty when I get going, especially if it’s about work nonsense or why software updates always break the one thing nobody tested.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed feeling unable to sit still or moving noticeably slower. Not something that’s jumped out at me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that others have commented on you seeming slowed down or, on the contrary, unusually restless or agitated lately?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think anyone’s said that to me. My wife might say I look tired sometimes, but slowed down or agitated—no, not really.\n\nAt work, people comment on all kinds of things, usually when something’s broken five minutes before a meeting, but I can’t think of anyone saying I’m moving slow or acting restless. So… no, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like life isn’t worth living or had thoughts about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: No… I mean, I don’t think so. That’s not really where my mind goes.\n\nI can get worn down by things, sure—everybody does, especially when work feels like it’s just one long chain of passwords expiring and people needing things yesterday—but thoughts about harming myself, no. And “life isn’t worth living”… I don’t know, that sounds pretty strong. I don’t think I’ve been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep—like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or sleeping more than usual even when you don’t feel tired?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been… not great, I guess. I usually fall asleep okay, so it’s not like I’m lying there for hours at the start.\n\nBut I’ll wake up around four in the morning, and then my brain just clicks on. It’s already sorting through some server issue, or a compliance deadline, or some ridiculous thing from work where nobody reads the email until it’s too late. And then I’m just lying there, half-awake, waiting for the alarm.\n\nSo technically I slept, but it doesn’t really feel like the night counted, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood and the lack of enjoyment in things have been present most of the day, nearly every day, for the past several months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around crying or anything like that, but there’s been this sort of gray, flat feeling most days for a long time now.\n\nIt’s like even things I used to look forward to don’t really land the same. Fishing, for example—I used to get the gear ready the night before, check the weather, all that. Now it’s more like, “Well, I said I was going, so I guess I’ll go.” Same with tending the tomatoes or going to a church concert. Those should be nice things, and they are, technically, but they feel more like items on a checklist than something I’m really enjoying.\n\nSo… most days, yes. It’s just been kind of muted.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant weight loss or a decrease in your appetite over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, though I didn’t really think of it as a “weight loss” thing at first.\n\nMy appetite’s been kind of off more days than not. I’ll get tied up with something at work and just work straight through lunch without really noticing, which is easy to do when you’ve got three different people asking why something won’t log in and nobody remembers what they changed. Then dinner comes around and I’ll sit there and kind of pick at it.\n\nMy wife noticed before I did, honestly. She said my pants were looking looser, and I’m not trying to lose weight or anything. So… yeah, probably some decrease in appetite and maybe some weight coming off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble focusing, making decisions, or thinking clearly during the day, more often than not?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it trouble thinking clearly, exactly. I mean, I can still do the work, and with computers there’s always some day where you’re staring at a screen thinking, “Why on earth would that setting be there?” Whoever designs some of these systems must not actually use them, you know?\n\nBut… decisions, focusing… I don’t know. Some days I do feel like I’m slower to get started, but I also wonder if that’s just normal at my age or just being tired of the same problems. I haven’t really noticed anything dramatic, I guess. Nothing where I’d say I can’t concentrate at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely lost interest in doing things you used to enjoy, like fishing or gardening, even feeling like there's no point in doing them anymore?\n\nPatient: Completely lost interest… I don’t know if I’d say completely. I still do the things sometimes. I’ll still go fishing, or go out and fuss with the tomatoes, or sit through a church concert if that’s on the calendar.\n\nBut for a long time now it’s had this gray, flat feeling to it. Like I’m doing the activity, but the part of me that used to look forward to it just isn’t really there. Fishing used to be a whole thing for me, getting ready and thinking about where to go, and now it’s more like… well, I said I’d go, so I go.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say “no point” exactly. It’s more that those things feel like chores or checklist items instead of something I actually enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt like doing things you used to enjoy wasn’t even worth the effort, or you didn’t see the point in doing them at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there have been times like that, I suppose. Not like I’m sitting there making some big declaration that fishing is pointless or anything, but it’s more… I look at the rods in the garage, or I think about going out to the tomatoes, and it just feels like another thing I’m supposed to do.\n\nIt’s been this gray, flat sort of feeling for a long time now, most days. Even stuff I used to look forward to — fishing, messing with the garden, going to a church concert — it can feel more like checking something off a list than actually enjoying it.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, well, if I’m just going to stand there and feel the same anyway, why bother? But then sometimes I still go, because it’s on the calendar or someone expects me to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
